The Elegant Joint Measurement is Non-Classical in the Triangle Network
Victor Gitton, Renato Renner

Abstract
When quantum systems are shared by multiple parties in a network, the measurement outcomes of the parties can exhibit non-classical correlations, i.e., correlations that cannot be obtained if the parties shared classical systems instead. This phenomenon is known as quantum nonlocality and is typically demonstrated in the Bell scenario. However, the Bell scenario is fundamentally simpler to investigate than general networks, since the latter come with non-convex optimization problems that are often intractable. The triangle network is one of the simplest networks exhibiting this non-convexity due to the presence of three independent sources. Although some special cases of quantum nonlocality are known in the triangle network, general methods to certify classical incompatibility are still lacking, which suggests that our understanding of networks is still rather limited. For instance, the Elegant Joint Measurement (EJM) distribution is a simple and highly-symmetric outcome distribution that can be obtained with quantum systems and measurements in the triangle network. This distribution was conjectured to be non-classical eight years ago. In this article, we provide the first proof of non-classicality of the EJM distribution. To do so, we show how to combine inflation, a causal inference technique, with powerful symmetry reductions and Frank-Wolfe algorithms for large-scale optimization. We then use these methods to obtain computer-assisted proofs of non-classicality in exact arithmetic.



1
Introduction

Figure 1
:
The triangle network features three observers (yellow squares), connected by three independent bipartite sources (green circles): Alice (
\mathrm{A}
) has access to the
\beta,\gamma
sources, while Bob (
\mathrm{B}
) has access to
\gamma,\alpha
and Charlie (
\mathrm{C}
) to
\alpha,\beta
.

Causal inference.

The idea underlying causal inference
[
1
,
2
]
is to abstract away an experiment and only retain minimal information about the way the systems of the experiment can influence each other.
This information is captured by a directed graph, also known as a causal graph, whose vertices describe the systems of the experiment, and whose directed edges indicate causal relations.
Consider for instance the triangle network represented in
Fig.

1
: it describes an experiment in which three bipartite systems, referred to as sources and denoted
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, may be measured by three parties named Alice, Bob and Charlie.
An important premise is that if a connection between two vertices (e.g., a source and a party) is absent from the causal graph, then this means that there is no way for the first vertex to directly influence the second.
For instance, in the triangle network of
Fig.

1
, the parties cannot send each other messages.
A causal graph can be equipped with quantum or classical causal models.
In a classical causal model in the triangle network, the sources can for instance be pairs of fair dice.
The parties could then apply an arbitrary processing of the dice they receive to measure them; they may for instance output the sum of the two dice they receive.
In a quantum causal model, the sources can be pairs of photons produced by optical excitations of a crystal.
The parties can then apply arbitrary quantum measurements of the polarization of these photons using polarizing filters and photon detectors.
This article will be primarily concerned with the problem of causal compatibility that asks about the existence of a causal model that reproduces the statistics of the scenario.
For instance, given a distribution
p(a,b,c)
over the outcomes of Alice, Bob and Charlie in the triangle network, is there a classical causal model (e.g., a dice distribution) or a quantum causal model (e.g., some quantum states for the photons) that gives rise to this
p(a,b,c)
?
Other causal problems include that of causal inference or that of counterfactual reasoning.
Reasoning about causes and effects is generally important in scientific studies, including physics — see for instance a recent application of causal inference in astrophysics research
[
3
]
.

Quantum nonlocality.

One of the most important results of quantum theory, Bell’s theorem
[
4
]
, is nowadays considered a central result of quantum causality
[
5
]
.
Bell’s theorem, in a causal language, states that there exist a causal graph and associated statistics that can be modelled using a quantum causal model, but that cannot be modelled using a classical causal model.
This feature was historically called quantum nonlocality, and has a rich landscape of associated experiments, mathematical techniques and applications to quantum information, communication and cryptography
[
6
]
.
We note that the problem of classical causal compatibility in Bell-like scenarios, where a single source is shared by all relevant parties, is a convex problem, enabling a number of relatively simple techniques for certifying classical incompatibility
[
6
]
.
The case of quantum causal compatibility in Bell-like scenarios is also relatively well-understood thanks to the Navascués-Pironio-Acín (NPA) hierarchy
[
7
,
8
]
.

Causal networks.

The result of Bell eventually opened the door to studying the differences between quantum and classical causal models in more general causal graphs.
Generalizing the case of the triangle network,
causal networks
are a natural class of causal scenarios for studying these differences
[
9
,
10
]
.
A causal network features a layer of sources connected in an arbitrary way to a layer of parties,
but where no pair of sources are connected to one another, and no pair of parties are connected to one another.
A number of examples of quantum nonlocality have been obtained in simple causal networks
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
,
and experimental implementations of such protocols are starting to be within reach of modern quantum experiments
[
16
,
17
,
18
]
.

A notable challenge when studying causal modelling in general networks is the mathematical difficulty to characterize the set of possible causal models, whether classical or quantum.
Certifying quantum and classical causal compatibility in general networks typically involves some sort of sampling method,
in which one iteratively invokes a causal model, computes the associated statistics and checks whether they are at least close to the target statistics.
Such sampling procedures have been used in the case of classical models in the triangle network via analytical methods
[
19
,
20
]
or via machine learning techniques such as neural networks
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
.
Certifying causal incompatibility is generally more challenging, since, for this purpose, one requires a way to say that no causal model can produce the desired statistics,
and there are, in some sense, many such causal models.
It is occasionally possible to compute analytical bounds that constrain the set of compatible statistics
[
23
]
.
There also exist analytical and numerical methods for this purpose using entropic constraints stemming directly from information theory
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
, which exist in different flavors depending on whether one wishes to constrain classical or quantum statistics.
Constraining quantum statistics in networks is also possible by adapting the NPA hierarchy to this setting
[
28
]
.

Figure 2
:

A fanout inflation of the triangle network of
Fig.

1
, consisting of two copies of each source and four copies of each party.
This inflation characterizes classical causal models in the triangle network: the fanning-out of each source indicate that the corresponding classical value sent by the source is copied and broadcast to the parties the source is connected to.
Note that all the edges of the graph start from a source and end at a party (we draw some edges behind the parties for convenience).

Inflation.

The most general method known to date for certifying classical or quantum incompatibility in networks is that of
inflation
.
The earliest results that can be meaningfully associated to inflation go back to de Finetti theorems, whether in the classical
[
29
,
30
]
or quantum
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
form.
The idea of inflation is to substitute the complex problem of causal compatibility on the original causal network with an easier problem, although the latter takes place on a larger causal network called an inflation of the network.
The inflation network is a network that consists of copies of the sources and of the parties of the original network, which can be interpreted operationally as obtaining an identical copy of the device implementing a source or a party, and connecting it to the other devices according to the inflation graph.
As an example, an inflation of the triangle network is presented in
Fig.

2
.
The inflation compatibility problem is not to check the existence of quantum or classical statistics that are compatible with the inflation network: this would be harder than solving the causal compatibility problem on the original network.
Instead, the idea of inflation compatibility is to check for the existence of statistics that have the same symmetries as the inflation graph, and that correctly reduce to the statistics of the original causal network when taking appropriate marginals.

The inflations characterizing classical networks are called fanout inflations
[
34
]
,
since they involve fanning-out the sources in the inflation graph as illustrated in
Fig.

2
: the fanning-out represents copying the values sent out by classical sources and sending the copies to the respective parties, something impossible in quantum theory due to the no-cloning theorem
[
35
]
.
To account for quantum sources, one can consider quantum theory merely from the perspective of its non-signaling properties, which even future theories of nature are expected to satisfy
[
19
,
36
,
37
]
.
To better characterize quantum theory out of non-signaling theories, it is possible to combine the ideas of the NPA hierarchy and of inflation, resulting in the so-called quantum inflation technique
[
38
,
39
]
.
In the case of classical causal scenarios, the inflation technique is known to provide a complete convergent characterization of classically compatible distributions
[
40
]
.
This surprising result means that copyable information, as characterized by fanout inflations, is information that admits a classical causal explanation.
The convergence of the quantum inflation technique remains unknown, although some progress has been made in that direction
[
41
]
.

The inflation method results in linear program (LP) or semidefinite program (SDP) relaxations which are usually solved by handing them over to a numerical solver.
However, the large inflation problems that characterize well the set of compatible distributions often require too much computational resources to be solved.
There is thus a practical limitation to the scope of applicability of inflation due to these computational challenges.
Overall, we still lack general techniques to prove classical causal incompatibility in networks.
Even the problem of certifying the classical incompatibility of a distribution
p(a,b,c)
with the triangle network, arguably one of the simplest networks, remains difficult.
Developing such techniques is a necessary step to characterize the differences between quantum and classical causal models in networks, without which finding quantum protocols that perform better than their classical counterparts in such networks remains a distant goal.
Furthermore, such techniques are also necessary in classical causal inference where one assesses the compatibility of a distribution with different causal graphs.

The EJM distribution.

One of the driving open problems in quantum nonlocality research was the case of the Elegant Joint Measurement (EJM) distribution
[
42
,
43
]
.
This is a simple and highly-symmetric distribution admitting a quantum causal model in the triangle network.
However, prior to this work, the EJM distribution had resisted a proof of existence or inexistence of a classical causal model, despite growing indications that no such classical causal model exists
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
.
Interestingly, the EJM distribution comes from a measurement that admits a simple characterization in terms of entanglement-assisted local measurements
[
44
]
.

Overview of the results.

We now present an overview of the contents of the article together with informal descriptions of our main results.
In
Section

2
, we introduce the necessary causal modelling background and present the EJM distribution.
In
Section

3
, we present the inflation problem that we will consider: this is a problem that, if it admits no solution, proves the classical causal incompatibility of the target distribution with the triangle network.

In
Section

4
, we present our first main result:
we show how to simplify the computational cost associated to solving the inflation problem by appealing to the symmetries of the inflation graph as well as the potential symmetries of the target distribution.
This kind of simplification is particularly useful for target distributions that have a lot of symmetries.
For instance, this is the case of the shared random bit (SRB)
[
19
,
28
,
34
,
45
]
or EJM distributions
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
42
,
43
,
46
]
.
However, the symmetrization procedure is also useful for distributions that do not exhibit any particular symmetries, since we always exploit the symmetries of the inflation graph.
We note that similar symmetrization procedures have already been carried out in the case of Bell scenarios
[
47
,
48
]
.

Section

5
is concerned with how to use and solve inflation problems in practice.
We present the concept of classical causal incompatibility certificates as a concrete way to make use of inflation problems.
The concept of certificate is quite general: any problem admitting a LP or SDP relaxation is eligible to a mathematical proof of infeasibility by means of verifying the validity of an infeasibility certificate.
In particular, inflation relaxations, whether fanout, non-fanout or quantum, can be shown to admit no solutions by means of such certificates.
A specificity of our work is that we demonstrate how to verify the validity of an inflation-based incompatibility certificate using exact (integer) arithmetic.
This gives us the ability to develop a computer-based method for proving causal incompatibility without suffering from floating-point arithmetic errors as was the case for most implementations of the inflation technique
[
15
,
22
,
45
]
.
We then discuss how to find incompatibility certificates.
We propose, for this purpose, to use a Frank-Wolfe algorithm, also known as conditional gradient method
[
49
]
.
The key idea allowing this use is to reformulate inflation problems as a geometric problem called polytope membership problem (PMP).
The Frank-Wolfe algorithm bypasses the computational overhead associated to using a general-purpose LP solver by exploiting the geometric structure of the PMP formulation of inflation problems.
Similar techniques had already been applied to Bell scenarios
[
48
,
50
]
, and we here demonstrate their usefulness in the context of more complicated network causal compatibility problems.
Although
Sections

4
and
5
focus on the triangle network for concreteness, these techniques can be adapted to simplify inflation problems in other causal networks, too.

We implemented the techniques of
Sections

4
and
5
in a publicly available codebase
[
51
]
.
In
Section

6
, we present applications of this code.
This includes consistency tests between our code and another independent code, as well as a resolution of the long-standing open problem regarding the incompatibility of the EJM distribution with the classical triangle network.

2
Causal modelling

2.1
Events and distributions

We start by introducing the notation that we will use to denote parties, events and distributions.
We
define

{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}=\{\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}\}

(2.1)

to be the parties of the triangle network of
Fig.

1
.
Then, consider a non-empty set
{\boldsymbol{A}}
, where each element
A\in{\boldsymbol{A}}
denotes a party.
Concretely, this could be the set
N
of parties of the triangle network, or a set of parties in an inflation graph as we will later describe.
For simplicity, we assume that every party has the same number of outcomes, denoted
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We
define the set of possible outcomes of each party as

\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}=\{0,\dots,n-1\}.

(2.2)

An
event
is then an assignment of outcomes to a set of parties
{\boldsymbol{A}}
.
We represent an event as a function
\omega:{\boldsymbol{A}}\to\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
, where each party
A\in{\boldsymbol{A}}
gets assigned the outcome
\omega(A)\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
.
The
set of all such events is denoted

\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{A}}=\{\omega:\boldsymbol{A}\to\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}\}.

(2.3)

The number of outcomes per party,
n
, is left implicit in the notation
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{A}}
.

A
probability distribution
describes the likelihood of each event in
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}}
.
We describe a probability distribution with a function
p:\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}}\to[0,1]
assigning the probability
p(\omega)\in[0,1]
to each event
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}}
.
The
set of all such probability distributions is then denoted

\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{\boldsymbol{A}}=\big\{p:\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\to\mathbb{R}\;\big|\;\forall\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{A}}:\ p(\omega)\geq 0\text{ and }\textstyle\sum_{\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{\boldsymbol{A}}}p(\omega)=1\big\}.

(2.4)

For instance, in the case of
{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}=\{\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}\}
, a distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
is to be understood as an outcome distribution for Alice, Bob and Charlie.
In this case, we write
p(a,b,c)
instead of
p(\omega)
, where
\omega(\mathrm{A})=a
,
\omega(\mathrm{B})=b
and
\omega(\mathrm{C})=c
.

2.2
The Elegant Joint Measurement

In this section, we present the Elegant Joint Measurement (EJM)
[
42
]
, a quantum measurement of two qubits introduced in the context of trying to obtain examples of quantum nonlocality in the triangle network.
The EJM is a two-qubit projective measurement consisting of four basis states with the same degree of entanglement.
These states have a high degree of symmetry: indeed, the Bloch vectors of the one-qubit reduced states form a regular tetrahedron in the Bloch sphere.
Interestingly, the EJM naturally reappeared as one of the few joint measurements that can be performed locally with the help of three Bell pairs
[
44
]
.

We consider the Hilbert space
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2}
describing two qubits.
We label the computational basis as
\{|00\rangle,|01\rangle,|10\rangle,|11\rangle\}
.
The EJM projectors can be written in this basis as
[
44
]

\displaystyle|\Phi^{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\phantom{+}\frac{e^{+i\pi/4}}{2}|00\rangle-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}|01\rangle+\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2}|11\rangle,

(2.5)

\displaystyle|\Phi^{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2}|00\rangle+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}|10\rangle-\frac{e^{+i\pi/4}}{2}|11\rangle,

\displaystyle|\Phi^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\phantom{+}\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2}|00\rangle+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}|10\rangle+\frac{e^{+i\pi/4}}{2}|11\rangle,

\displaystyle|\Phi^{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{+i\pi/4}}{2}|00\rangle-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}|01\rangle-\frac{e^{-i\pi/4}}{2}|11\rangle.

There are thus
n=4
outcomes per party.
We consider a quantum causal model in the triangle network where each source distributes two qubits in a singlet state

|\psi^{-}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(|01\rangle-|10\rangle\big),

(2.6)

and each party performs the EJM on the two qubits they receive.
The resulting distribution
p_{\textup{ejm}}\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{\{\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}\}}
can be computed via the Born rule:

p_{\textup{ejm}}(a,b,c)=\Big|\langle\Phi^{a}|_{\beta_{R}\gamma_{L}}\langle\Phi^{b}|_{\gamma_{R}\alpha_{L}}\langle\Phi^{c}|_{\alpha_{R}\beta_{L}}|\psi^{-}\rangle_{\alpha_{L}\alpha_{R}}|\psi^{-}\rangle_{\beta_{L}\beta_{R}}|\psi^{-}\rangle_{\gamma_{L}\gamma_{R}}\Big|^{2}.

(2.7)

It can be checked through a simple numerical calculation that this distribution reads explicitly

p_{\textup{ejm}}(a,b,c)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{25}{256}&\textup{ if }a=b=c,\\
\frac{5}{256}&\textup{ if }a\neq b\neq c\neq a,\\
\frac{1}{256}&\textup{ else.}\end{aligned}\right.

(2.8)

We see that
p_{\textup{ejm}}
is highly symmetric: it holds that

p_{\textup{ejm}}(a,b,c)=p_{\textup{ejm}}(b,a,c)=p_{\textup{ejm}}(a,c,b),

(2.9)

meaning that
p_{\textup{ejm}}
is symmetric under party exchanges, as well as
1
1

1

Here,
\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{4}
denotes the symmetric group of order
4
, i.e., the group of bijections of the set
\{0,1,2,3\}
.

\forall\sigma\in\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{4}:\ p_{\textup{ejm}}\big(\sigma(a),\sigma(b),\sigma(c)\big)=p_{\textup{ejm}}(a,b,c),

(2.10)

meaning that
p_{\textup{ejm}}
is symmetric under joint outcome relabelings.

2.3
Classical causal models

We now describe classical causal models in the triangle network, and how such a causal model is processed to obtain a probability distribution
p(a,b,c)
over the outcomes of Alice, Bob and Charlie.
A
response function
describes how a party processes their inputs to produce their outcome.
For instance, Alice receives the inputs
\beta
and
\gamma
, which we can assume to take values in
[0,1]
, to produce her outcome
a
(see
Fig.

1
).
Thus, the response function of Alice is represented by the conditional distribution
p_{\mathrm{A}}
taking values
p_{\mathrm{A}}(a|\beta,\gamma)\in[0,1]
and representing the probability with which Alice outputs
a\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
given her inputs
\beta,\gamma\in[0,1]
.
This conditional distribution must be normalized:

\sum_{a\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}}p_{\mathrm{A}}(a|\beta,\gamma)=1.

(2.11)

The response functions of Bob and Charlie,
p_{\mathrm{B}}(b|\gamma,\alpha)
and
p_{\mathrm{C}}(c|\alpha,\beta)
, satisfy analogous conditions.

A classical causal model in the triangle network consists of three response functions
p_{\mathrm{A}},p_{\mathrm{B}},p_{\mathrm{C}}
.
A distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
is classically compatible with the triangle network is there exists response functions
p_{\mathrm{A}},p_{\mathrm{B}},p_{\mathrm{C}}
such that, for all
a,b,c\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
,

p(a,b,c)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\alpha\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\beta\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\gamma\,p_{\mathrm{A}}(a|\beta,\gamma)\,p_{\mathrm{B}}(b|\gamma,\alpha)\,p_{\mathrm{C}}(c|\alpha,\beta).

(2.12)

The
set of all distributions classically compatible with the triangle network is denoted

\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}.

(2.13)

3
Inflation

In this section, we introduce a simple inflation problem that we will use in
Sections

4
and
5
to demonstrate our results.

3.1
Inflation graph

Consider the inflation graph of
Fig.

2
: this graph is obtained by taking two copies of each source and four copies of each party in the triangle network.
The size of this inflation is denoted
{z}=(2,2,2)
, since we take two copies of each source.
The inflation graph of
Fig.

2
was first introduced as the “web inflation” graph
[
34
]
and is used to constrain classical statistics in the triangle network
[
12
,
16
,
22
,
45
]
.
This type of inflation graph can be generalized to account for an arbitrary inflation size
({z}_{\alpha},{z}_{\beta},{z}_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, meaning
{z}_{\alpha}
copies of the
\alpha
source, etc.
[
40
,
52
]
.

The
inflation parties appearing in
Fig.

2
are denoted

{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}=\{\mathrm{A}_{jk}\}_{j,k\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}}\cup\{\mathrm{B}_{jk}\}_{j,k\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}}\cup\{\mathrm{C}_{jk}\}_{j,k\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}}.

(3.1)

The idea of the inflation relaxation is that we will look for a distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
over the outcomes of the inflation parties such that
q
satisfies certain properties related to the classical compatibility of a distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
with the triangle network.
A distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
is a function that assigns the probability
q(\omega)\in[0,1]
to each inflation event
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
.
Mimicking the arrangement of the inflation graph in
Fig.

2
, we will use the following matrix-like notation to represent inflation events:

\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\mapsto\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to143.11pt{\vbox to62.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.70921pt\lower-52.63756pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

(3.4)

3.2
Source symmetries

We will impose symmetry constraints on inflation distributions.
We
denote the symmetric group with

\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{k}=\{f:\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}k\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}\to\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}k\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}\textup{ bijective}\}.

(3.5)

Then,
the source symmetry group is defined as

\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}=\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{2}\times\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{2}\times\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{2},

(3.6)

and we denote its elements as
\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
.
Source symmetries act as follows on inflation parties:

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathrm{A}_{jk})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{A}_{\zeta_{1}(j)\zeta_{2}(k)},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathrm{B}_{jk})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{B}_{\zeta_{2}(j)\zeta_{0}(k)},

\displaystyle\zeta(\mathrm{C}_{jk})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{C}_{\zeta_{0}(j)\zeta_{1}(k)}.

The idea is that the element
\zeta_{0}
permutes the source
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}
, which can be cancelled out by exchanging the
k
index of
\mathrm{B}_{jk}
and the
j
index of
\mathrm{C}_{jk}
, etc.
Then, source symmetries act on inflation events
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
according to

\zeta(\omega)=\omega\circ\zeta^{-1}\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

(3.8)

For instance, consider the case of
\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
, where
\zeta_{0}=(01)
(meaning,
\zeta_{0}(0)=1
,
\zeta_{0}(1)=0
, corresponding to a swap of the sources
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}
),
\zeta_{1}=(01)
(swap the sources
\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{1}
) and
\zeta_{2}=\mathrm{id}
(leave the
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
sources unchanged).
Then, using the representation of
Eq.

3.2
, we have that

\zeta=\big(\zeta_{0}=(01),\zeta_{1}=(01),\zeta_{2}=\mathrm{id}\big):\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to59.32pt{\vbox to43.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.72192pt\lower-36.84692pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

(3.9)

Finally, the source symmetry
\zeta\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
act on inflation distributions
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
by sending it to
\zeta(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
which is defined as

\big[\zeta(q)\big](\omega)=q\big(\zeta^{-1}(\omega)\big)=q(\omega\circ\zeta)\in[0,1]

(3.10)

where
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
is any inflation event.
The idea is that the party
A
in
q
is now “renamed”
\zeta(A)
in
\zeta(q)
.

Figure 3
:

The subgraph of the parties
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}=\{\mathrm{A}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{00},\mathrm{C}_{00}\}
and
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}=\{\mathrm{A}_{11},\mathrm{B}_{11},\mathrm{C}_{11}\}

together with their parent sources
in the inflation graph of
Fig.

2
,
We observe the
d
-separation
[
1
]
of
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
, i.e., there is no source connecting both a party in
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}
and a party in
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
).
We also observe that
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
are so-called injectable sets
[
34
]
, i.e., they are connected in a manner equivalent to the triangle network of
Fig.

1
).

3.3
Inflation problem

Let us now formulate the inflation relaxation that we will consider.
We will be looking for an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying some linear constraints related to the classical compatibility of a target distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
with the triangle network.
We will need the following two sets of inflation parties to form the appropriate inflation marginals:

{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}=\{\mathrm{A}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{00},\mathrm{C}_{00}\},\qquad{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}=\{\mathrm{A}_{11},\mathrm{B}_{11},\mathrm{C}_{11}\}.

(3.11)

These sets of inflation parties together with their parent sources are represented in
Fig.

3
.

Marginals.

We will denote with
q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
the marginal of an inflation distribution
q
on the parties
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}=\{\mathrm{A}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{00},\mathrm{C}_{00},\mathrm{A}_{11},\mathrm{B}_{11},\mathrm{C}_{11}\}
.
This marginal is defined as usual:
2
2

2

Extracting a marginal event from a complete inflation event is denoted with the restriction of a function: we denote with
\omega_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}:{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}\to\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
the restriction (marginal event) of the function (“full” event)
\omega:{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}\to\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
.
for each marginal event
\mu\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
,

q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}(\mu)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\\
\omega_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\mu\end{subarray}}q(\omega).

(3.12)

Explicitly, identifying
\mu
with
a_{00},b_{00},c_{00},a_{11},b_{11},c_{11}\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
,

q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to73.56pt{\vbox to44.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 19.101pt\lower-37.18114pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

(3.13)

It
will be useful to explicitly manipulate the linear map
\hyperlink{target:margmap}{\mathsf{M}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
that implements this marginalization: we let

\hyperlink{target:margmap}{\mathsf{M}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}(q)=q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-0.31917pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-10.2778pt}{-13.18085pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.33713pt}{-12.43085pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{}
{{}{}}{}
{{}{}}{}
{{}{}}{}
{{}{}}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 20.67 6.89 L 20.67 -48.23 L -6.89 -48.23 L -6.89 6.89 L 48.23 6.89 L 48.23 -20.67 L -6.89 -20.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\right)=p({\color[rgb]{0.707,0,0}a_{0}},{\color[rgb]{0.707,0,0}b_{0}},{\color[rgb]{0.707,0,0}c_{0}})\cdot p({\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0}a_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0}b_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4688,0}c_{1}}).

(3.15)

This
constraint can be reformulated as a linear constraint on the inflation distribution: we define the constraint map

\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}=\hyperlink{target:margmap}{\mathsf{M}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}-p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}

(3.16)

as the linear map such that
4
4

4

Seeing this map written as in
Eq.

3.16
, it looks like an affine map, not a linear map.
However, the idea is that the constant term
p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
should be thought of as
p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\cdot\sum_{\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}}q(\omega)
, which is equivalent thanks to the normalization of
q
, and can clearly be extended to a linear map over the whole vector space in which
q
lives.

\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)=\hyperlink{target:margmap}{\mathsf{M}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}(q)-p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}-p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(3.17)

This allows us to rewrite

q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)=0.

(3.18)

We can now define the explicit inflation problem that we will be looking at in this example.
This kind of inflation problem is standard in the literature
[
12
,
16
,
22
,
34
,
40
,
45
]
.

Definition 3.1

(Explicit inflation problem)
.

We
define the set

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompati}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{expl}}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}

(3.19)

of triangle network distributions
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
such that there exists an inflation distribution

q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying

symmetry constraint:

\displaystyle\text{for all }\zeta\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}},\ \zeta(q)=q,

(3.20a)

marginal constraint:

\displaystyle\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(3.20b)

Importantly, the inflation problem is a relaxation of the classical causal compatibility problem, as shown in the following proposition.
Thus, if we can prove that the inflation problem of
Def–.˝

3.1
has no solution, i.e., that given
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
, there does not exist an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying the constraints of
Eq.

3.20
, then this proves that the distribution
p
is classically incompatible with the triangle network, i.e., that
p\notin\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}
.
If instead
p\in\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompati}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{expl}}}}
, then the inflation problem is inconclusive with respect to proving the compatibility of
p
, i.e.,
p
may or may not be classically compatible with the triangle network.

Proposition 3.2
.

It holds that

\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompati}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{expl}}}}.

(3.21)

Proof.

The idea is that if
p\in\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}
, then there exists response functions
p_{\mathrm{A}}
,
p_{\mathrm{B}}
and
p_{\mathrm{C}}
satisfying
Eq.

2.12
.
Then, we can define
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
as the distribution that one obtains in the graph of
Fig.

2
if all Alices use the response function
p_{\mathrm{A}}
, all Bobs use
p_{\mathrm{B}}
and all Charlies use
p_{\mathrm{C}}
: for all
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
, we define

q(\omega)=\int\mathrm{d}\alpha_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\beta_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma_{1}\,\prod_{j,k\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}}p_{\mathrm{A}}(a_{jk}|\beta_{j},\gamma_{k})p_{\mathrm{B}}(b_{jk}|\gamma_{j},\alpha_{k})p_{\mathrm{C}}(c_{jk}|\alpha_{j},\beta_{k}),

(3.22)

where all integration variables are integrated on the
[0,1]
interval, as in the original local model of
p
, and where we defined the outcomes

a_{jk}=\omega(\mathrm{A}_{jk}),\quad b_{jk}=\omega(\mathrm{B}_{jk}),\quad c_{jk}=\omega(\mathrm{C}_{jk}).

(3.23)

It is then straightforward to show that this
q
satisfies the constraints of
Eq.

3.20
.
Intuitively, the source symmetry constraint
\zeta(q)=q
follows from
Eq.

3.22
since the latter incorporates the symmetries of the inflation graph of
Fig.

2
.
The fact that
q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
factorizes as
q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
follows from the
d
-separation
[
1
]
of the set of parties
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
in the inflation graph of
Fig.

2
, as represented in
Fig.

3
.
Finally, the fact that
q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i}}=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i}}
follows from the fact that the ancestral subgraph of the inflation graph of
Fig.

2
generated by the parties
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i}
is isomorphic to the triangle network (see also
Fig.

3
).
This characteristic means that
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i}
is a so-called injectable set
[
34
]
.
∎

4
Symmetry reduction

We now want to simplify the above inflation problem by exploiting the symmetries of the target distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
as well as the symmetries of the inflation graph of
Fig.

2
.
The idea is to impose, without loss of generality, additional symmetry constraints on the inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
to reduce the computational complexity associated to inflation problems.

It is natural to consider target distributions
p
that have symmetries.
For instance, in the context of quantum nonlocality, one frequently encounters triangle distributions
p
that arise from a quantum model in which all parties perform the same measurement and all sources are prepared in the same state
[
11
,
43
]
.
The resulting distributions
p
will exhibit a symmetry under exchanges of the parties as in
Eq.

2.9
.
This is in particular the case of the EJM distribution (see
Section

2.2
), but the EJM has an even larger symmetry group: it is also invariant under joint permutations of the outcomes as in
Eq.

2.10
.
This type of outcome symmetries also appear in the noisy shared random bit distribution
[
19
,
28
,
34
,
45
]
that we will also investigate in
Section

6.1
.

However, the symmetrization procedure that we develop in this section is also useful for a general distribution
p
that exhibits no symmetries (e.g., a distribution coming from noisy experimental statistics).
Indeed, even when
p
has no particular symmetries, the inflation distribution
q
is always constrained to be symmetric under source exchanges, and we also exploit this symmetry to reduce the computational complexity of the inflation problem.

4.1
Party and outcome symmetries

Party symmetries.

We
define the party symmetry group as

\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}=\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{3}.

(4.1)

A party symmetry
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}
acts on inflation parties by exchanging the names of the parties and transposing their copy indices.
To manipulate party symmetries, it is convenient to label the names of parties with
0,1,2
instead of
\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}
.
We thus define, for all
j,k\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
,

(0,j,k)\coloneqq\mathrm{A}_{jk},\quad(1,j,k)\coloneqq\mathrm{B}_{jk},\quad(2,j,k)\coloneqq\mathrm{C}_{jk}.

(4.2)

A party symmetry
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}
then acts on an inflation party
A=(t,j,k)\in{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
as follows:
5
5

5

The signature
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)
is defined in
Section

A.1.1
.

\tau(A)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\tau(t),j,k),\textup{ if }\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=+1,\\
&(\tau(t),k,j),\textup{ if }\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=-1.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.3)

For instance, if
\tau=(01)
, meaning, “exchange party 0 (Alice) and party 1 (Bob)”, then

\tau(\mathrm{A}_{jk})=\mathrm{B}_{kj},\quad\tau(\mathrm{B}_{jk})=\mathrm{A}_{kj},\quad\tau(\mathrm{C}_{jk})=\mathrm{C}_{kj}.

(4.4)

If instead
\tau=(012)
, meaning, “cyclically exchange Alice to Bob to Charlie to Alice”, then

\tau(\mathrm{A}_{jk})=\mathrm{B}_{jk},\quad\tau(\mathrm{B}_{jk})=\mathrm{C}_{jk},\quad\tau(\mathrm{C}_{jk})=\mathrm{A}_{jk}.

(4.5)

The reason for transposing the indices in this way is to have appropriate commutation relations between
PartyGrp
and
SrcGrp

(see
Lemma

A.3
).
Party symmetries act on inflation events
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
analogously to the source symmetries:

\tau(\omega)=\omega\circ\tau^{-1}\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

(4.6)

For instance, using the representation of
Eq.

3.2
,

\tau=(01):\ \vbox{\hbox{\hbox to59.32pt{\vbox to43.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.72192pt\lower-36.84692pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

(4.7)

Party symmetries then act on inflation distributions
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
analogously to the source symmetries:

\big[\tau(q)\big](\omega)=q\big(\tau^{-1}(\omega)\big)=q(\omega\circ\tau)\in[0,1].

(4.8)

Party symmetries also act naturally on a target distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
: for all
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
,

\big[\tau(p)\big](a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})=p(a_{\tau(0)},a_{\tau(1)},a_{\tau(2)}).

(4.9)

Outcome symmetries.

The
outcome symmetry group is defined as

\hyperlink{target:outgroup}{\textup{{OutGrp}}}=\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{n}.

(4.10)

Outcome symmetries act on inflation events by permuting the outcomes of all parties:

\sigma(\omega)=\sigma\circ\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}.

(4.11)

An outcome symmetry
\sigma
then act on an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
and a target distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
as

\displaystyle\forall\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}:

\displaystyle\big[\sigma(q)\big](\omega)=q\big(\sigma^{-1}(\omega)\big)=q(\sigma^{-1}\circ\omega)\in[0,1],

(4.12)

\displaystyle\forall a,b,c\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}:

\displaystyle\big[\sigma(p)\big](a,b,c)=p\big(\sigma^{-1}(a),\sigma^{-1}(b),\sigma^{-1}(c)\big).

Inflation symmetry group.

We
can then define the full inflation symmetry group as

\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}=\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}\times\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}\times\hyperlink{target:outgroup}{\textup{{OutGrp}}}.

(4.13)

A symmetry
\iota={{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}
thus consists of a source symmetry, a party symmetry and an outcome symmetry, and acts accordingly: we let, for each inflation event
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,

\iota(\omega)=\zeta\circ\tau\circ\sigma(\omega)=\sigma\circ\omega\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta^{-1}\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

(4.14)

For instance, chaining
Eqs.

3.9
,
4.7
and
4.11
, we have that

{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 20.67 6.89 L 20.67 -48.23 L -6.89 -48.23 L -6.89 6.89 L 48.23 6.89 L 48.23 -20.67 L -6.89 -20.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}.

(4.16)

The action on an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
is then given by

\iota(q)=\zeta\circ\tau\circ\sigma(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

(4.17)

or explicitly,

\big[\iota(q)\big](\omega)=q\big(\iota^{-1}(\omega)\big)=q\big(\sigma^{-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta^{-1}(\omega)\big)=q\big(\sigma^{-1}\circ\omega\circ\zeta\circ\tau\big)\in[0,1].

(4.18)

The group operation of
InfGrp
and the commutation relation between the source, party and outcome symmetry group ensure that this construction is well-defined: see
Section

A.1.3
.

4.2
First symmetrization

The first symmetrization of the inflation problem consists in enforcing, without loss of generality, that the inflation distribution
q
is symmetric under source, party and outcome symmetries.
We
define the group of target distribution symmetries as

\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}=\big\{\iota={{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}\;\big|\;\tau\circ\sigma(p)=p\big\},

(4.19)

i.e., the inflation symmetries such that the party and outcome symmetry part leaves the target distribution invariant.
In the case of the Elegant Joint Measurement (
Eq.

2.8
), we have
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}=\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}},
since all party and outcome symmetries leave the EJM distribution invariant as shown in
Eqs.

2.9
and
2.10
.
For a distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
with no particular symmetries,
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}\simeq\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
.

The inflation problem of
Def–.˝

3.1
turns out to be equivalent to the following one,
where the marginal constraint remains the same, but the symmetry requirement is, in general, stronger than that of
Def–.˝

3.1
.

Definition 4.1
.

We
define the set

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompatii}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym1}}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}

(4.20)

of distributions
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
such that there exists an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying

extended symmetry constraint:

\displaystyle\forall\iota\in\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}},\ \iota(q)=q,

(4.21a)

marginal constraint:

\displaystyle\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(4.21b)

The equivalence statement, formalized in
, states that

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompati}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{expl}}}}=\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompatii}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym1}}}},

(4.22)

i.e., that given
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
, there exists
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying
Eq.

3.20
if and only if there exists
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying
Eq.

4.21
.
Since the symmetry requirement of
Eq.

4.21
is stronger than that of
Eq.

3.20
(on top of source symmetries, the inflation distribution needs to be symmetric under party and outcome symmetries), one direction is trivial.
The non-trivial direction is that a solution
q
to
Eq.

3.20
implies that
the
twirled distribution

q^{\prime}=\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=\frac{1}{|\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|}\sum_{\iota\in\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}}\iota(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}

(4.23)

is a valid solution to
Eq.

4.21
.
This is a consequence of the commutation (or rather, covariance) relations between the additional party and outcome symmetries with the source symmetries.

4.3
Second symmetrization

In the second symmetrization of the inflation problem, we will bring to light even more symmetries of the inflation problem by using group twirls.
We have already defined the group twirl
\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}
in
Eq.

4.23
.

Marginal symmetry.

We have imposed a symmetry requirement on the inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
in
Eq.

4.21a
, and this implies a symmetry on the output of
\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)
.
To
express this, we require the symmetries from
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}
that preserve the set
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}=\{\mathrm{A}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{00},\mathrm{C}_{00},\mathrm{A}_{11},\mathrm{B}_{11},\mathrm{C}_{11}\}
.
Any party symmetry in
PartyGrp
is allowed, but only two source permutations in
SrcGrp
are allowed: the trivial source permutation, and the source permutation that swaps
\alpha_{0}
with
\alpha_{1}
,
\beta_{0}
with
\beta_{1}
and
\gamma_{0}
with
\gamma_{1}
.
Thus, we define

{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}=\big\{\iota={{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}\;\big|\;\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{0},\zeta_{0})\text{ with }\zeta_{0}\in\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{2}\text{ and }\tau\circ\sigma(p)=p\big\}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}.

(4.24)

In the case of the EJM distribution,
p=p_{\textup{ejm}}
(
Eq.

2.8
), we have

{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}=\big\{(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{0},\zeta_{0})\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}\;\big|\;\zeta_{0}\in\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{2}\big\}\times\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}\times\hyperlink{target:outgroup}{\textup{{OutGrp}}}.

(4.25)

Marginal actions.

The symmetries of
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
are those symmetries that leave
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
invariant, which allows us to define an action of
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
on marginal inflation events
\mu\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
as well as marginal inflation distributions
q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
.
These actions are the natural extensions of those on full inflation events and distributions.
For instance,

(4.26)

and thus

{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 20.67 6.89 L 20.67 -48.23 L -6.89 -48.23 L -6.89 6.89 L 48.23 6.89 L 48.23 -20.67 L -6.89 -20.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
