\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}.

(4.27)

We
can then define the constraint group twirl

\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}(q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})=\frac{1}{|\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}|}\sum_{\iota\in{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}}\iota(q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}).

(4.28)

This allows us to define the following inflation problem.

Definition 4.2
.

We
define the set

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompatiii}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym2}}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}

(4.29)

of distributions
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
such that there exists an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
satisfying

\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0.

(4.30)

As shown in
, the inflation problems of
Defs–.˝

4.1
and
4.2
are equivalent, i.e.,

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompatii}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym1}}}}=\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompatiii}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym2}}}}.

(4.31)

The idea is the following: instead of enforcing that
q
has to be symmetric (as in
Eq.

4.21a
), we let
q
be non-symmetric
but we only look at the symmetrized
\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)
in the marginal constraint.
Concretely, this means that instead of enforcing

q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)=0,

(4.32)

we only enforce that

\big[\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)\big]_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0.

(4.33)

Then, if
\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0
, it also holds that
\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0
by linearity of
\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}
.
The non-trivial part is that the converse is also true, and this essentially relies on the commutation relation between
\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}
and
\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}
.

4.4
Third symmetrization

In the third symmetrization, we wish to exploit the symmetries of the inflation problem of
Def–.˝

4.1
to reduce the number of scalar variables and scalar constraints.
Similar ideas were already exploited in the context of finding Bell inequalities
[
47
,
48
]
.
To exploit the symmetries of the inflation problem, we need to consider the orbits generated by the groups
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
and
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}
.

Table 1
:

A graphical visualization of the orbits of inflation events that we consider in our symmetrization procedure.
See in particular
Eqs.

4.35
and
4.37
for details about this construction, and
Eq.

3.2
for the representation that we choose for inflation events.

Orbits.

We first consider the orbits of marginal events.
We define the orbit of the marginal inflation event
\mu\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
under the action of the constraint symmetry group
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
as

{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}(\omega)=\big\{\iota(\omega)\big\}_{\iota\in{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(4.34)

The
set of all orbits is denoted

\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}=\big\{{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}(\omega)\;\big|\;\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\big\}.

(4.35)

We pick an ordering of the set
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
to define the representative
\hyperlink{target:repr}{\lfloor}o\hyperlink{target:repr}{\rfloor}
of an orbit
o\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
as the element of the orbit
o
that is lowest in the ordering.
The
set of representatives of the orbits is then denoted

\hyperlink{target:repr}{\lfloor}\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}\hyperlink{target:repr}{\rfloor}=\big\{\hyperlink{target:repr}{\lfloor}o\hyperlink{target:repr}{\rfloor}\;\big|\;o\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}\big\}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(4.36)

Similarly,
in the case of “full” inflation events, we define the representatives of the orbits of inflation events
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
under the symmetry group
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}
as follows:

\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}=\hyperlink{target:repr}{\lfloor}\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}\hyperlink{target:repr}{\rfloor}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

(4.37)

In the case of the EJM distribution,
p=p_{\textup{ejm}}
(
Eq.

2.8
),
the set
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
contains
n^{6}
events, so since for the EJM
n=4
, there are
4^{\prime}096
such events.
With the group
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
of the EJM distribution (see
Eq.

4.25
), there are only
|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}|=33
orbits.
Regarding the set of inflation events, there are
n^{12}\approx 17\cdot 10^{6}
events in
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
.
For the EJM distribution, there are only
15^{\prime}418
events in
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}=\hyperlink{target:repr}{\lfloor}\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}\hyperlink{target:repr}{\rfloor}
.
We represent these orbits in
Table

1
.
6
6

6

In
Table

1
, we picked the ordering of
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
corresponding to the lexicographic ordering of the list of outcomes

\omega(\mathrm{A}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{00},\mathrm{C}_{00},\mathrm{A}_{11},\mathrm{B}_{11},\mathrm{C}_{11})
,
and the ordering of
\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
corresponds to the lexicographic ordering of the list of outcomes

\omega(\mathrm{A}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{00},\mathrm{C}_{00},\mathrm{B}_{01},\mathrm{C}_{10},\mathrm{A}_{10},\mathrm{C}_{01},\mathrm{C}_{11},\mathrm{A}_{01},\mathrm{B}_{10},\mathrm{A}_{11},\mathrm{B}_{11})
,
where we write
\omega(A_{0},A_{1},\dots)
as a shortcut for the list of outcomes
(\omega(A_{0}),\omega(A_{1}),\dots)
.

The point of the reduced set of events
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
is that we will eventually consider inflation distributions
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
that only have support on
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
.
We
thus define

\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}=\big\{q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\;\big|\;\forall\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\setminus\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}:\ q(\omega)=0\big\}=\mathrm{conv}\big\{\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega}\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\;\big|\;\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}\big\},

(4.38)

where the latter expression refers to a convex hull of deterministic distributions.
7
7

7

Explicitly,
for all
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,
we define the deterministic distribution
\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega}\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
as:
for all
\omega^{\prime}\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,

\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega}(\omega^{\prime})=1
if
\omega^{\prime}=\omega
and
0
else.

Reduced constraint space.

Recall that the inflation constraint of
Def–.˝

4.2
is given by

\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0.

(4.39)

This
is an equality in the vector space of functions that assigns numbers to marginal inflation events, which we denote as

\hyperlink{target:vecset}{\vphantom{V}\smash{V\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\{v:\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}\}.

(4.40)

However, since the group twirl
\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}
is a projector, we do not need to impose the constraint on the full
\hyperlink{target:vecset}{\vphantom{V}\smash{V\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
.
Instead,
it is sufficient to consider the following “subspace”:

\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}=\{v:(\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}})\to\mathbb{R}\}.

(4.41)

We
define the isometric reduction map

\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}:\hyperlink{target:vecset}{\vphantom{V}\smash{V\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\to\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}

(4.42)

that adds up the values assigned to the events in each orbit: for all
v\in\hyperlink{target:vecset}{\vphantom{V}\smash{V\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
, for all
o\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
, we define

\big[\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}(v)\big](o)=\sum_{\omega\in o}v(\omega).

(4.43)

Equipped with this reduction map to reduce the number of scalar constraints, and with the reduced set
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
(
Eq.

4.37
) of inflation events to reduce the number of scalar variables, we can now state the third and final symmetrized version of our inflation problem.
The idea is that we replace the “full” marginal constraint

\hyperlink{target:margtwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0,

(4.44)

corresponding to
|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}|
scalar constraints, with the “reduced” marginal constraint

\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}(q)=0,

(4.45)

corresponding to only
|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}|
scalar constraints.
It will be useful for the upcoming
Section

5
to give a short name to the corresponding linear map:
We
define

\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}=\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}.

(4.46)

Definition 4.3
.

We
define the set

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}

(4.47)

of distributions
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
such that there exists an inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}
(i.e., with support only on the reduced set of events
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
) satisfying

\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(q)=0.

(4.48)

As shown in
, the inflation problems of
Def–.˝

4.2
and
Def–.˝

4.3
are equivalent, i.e.,

\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompatiii}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym2}}}}=\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}.

(4.49)

Thus, overall, we have that
\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompati}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{expl}}}}=\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}
.

4.5
Computational improvements

Although the problems of
InfCompat

expl

and
InfCompat

sym3

are mathematically equivalent, the problem of
InfCompat

sym3

is much easier to handle computationally.
We will label a number of scalar variables as
n_{\textup{var}}\in\mathbb{N}
and a number of scalar constraints as
n_{\textup{cons}}\in\mathbb{N}
.
For instance, for the EJM distribution, the problem of
InfCompat

expl

has
8
8

8

The number of constraints corresponds to the marginal constraint of
Eq.

3.20b
only.
This would be even more if one implements the symmetry constraints of
Eq.

3.20a
as linear constraints.

\displaystyle n_{\textup{var}}

\displaystyle=|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}|=n^{|{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}|}=4^{12}\approx 17\cdot 10^{6}\textup{ (one for each $q(\omega)$, $\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}$)},

(4.50)

\displaystyle n_{\textup{cons}}

\displaystyle=\dim(\hyperlink{target:vecset}{\vphantom{V}\smash{V\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})=|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}|=n^{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}|}=4^{6}=4^{\prime}096.

On the other hand, in the formulation of
InfCompat

sym3

, the situation looks much better:

\displaystyle n_{\textup{var}}

\displaystyle=|\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}|=|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|=15^{\prime}418\textup{ (one for each $q(\omega)$, $\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}$)},

(4.51)

\displaystyle n_{\textup{cons}}

\displaystyle=\dim(\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}})=|\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}|=33.

This computational improvement gained by the symmetrization essentially scales with the size of
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}
(see
Eq.

4.19
).
Thus, the more symmetries the target distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
exhibits, the better the improvement.

Furthermore, the source symmetries of the inflation graph are always symmetries of the inflation problem, and these play a part in reducing the number of variables and constraints since they appear in
\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}
and
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
.
The larger the inflation graph, the more source symmetries there are: if we consider inflation graphs akin to the
(2,2,2)
inflation graph of
Fig.

2
but with a general inflation size
({z}_{\alpha},{z}_{\beta},{z}_{\gamma})

[
40
,
52
]
(corresponding to the number of copies of each source, which correspondingly updates the number of inflation parties), there are
|\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}|={z}_{0}!\cdot{z}_{1}!\cdot{z}_{2}!
source symmetries.
Thus, a larger inflation graph means that the symmetrization will be even more significant (irrespective of the symmetries of the target distribution
p
).

For the inflation problem that allows us to prove the classical incompatibility of the EJM distribution with the triangle network (see
Eq.

6.3
), the improvement looks as follows:
prior to symmetrization, in the formulation of
InfCompat

expl

, we would have had:

\displaystyle n_{\textup{var}}

\displaystyle=4^{20}\approx 1.1\cdot 10^{12},

(4.52)

\displaystyle n_{\textup{cons}}

\displaystyle=4^{10}+2\cdot 4^{9}\approx 1.6\cdot 10^{6}.

After the symmetrization, in the formulation of
InfCompat

sym3

, this gets improved to:

\displaystyle n_{\textup{var}}

\displaystyle\approx 2.4\cdot 10^{8},

(4.53)

\displaystyle n_{\textup{cons}}

\displaystyle=5^{\prime}780.

5
Solving inflation problems

We now explain how we go about solving inflation problems.
As described in
Section

4.5
, the symmetrization procedure that we employ helps us reduce the computational cost of running a given inflation problem.
Thus, this allows us to solve larger inflation problems (i.e., with larger inflation graphs and more constraints) compared to what was possible so far in the literature
[
12
,
16
,
22
,
34
,
45
]
.
However, the resulting inflation problems are still challenging to simply hand over to a commercial linear problem solver.
For instance, the numbers of
Eq.

4.53
are, to the best of our knowledge, beyond what commercial linear problem solvers and usual university clusters can handle.
The principal limitation is the memory required: given that after the symmetry reduction, there is not much sparsity in the linear problem matrix, storing this matrix would require storing roughly
2.4\cdot 10^{8}\cdot 5780\approx 1.4\cdot 10^{12}
numbers.
Even with
32
-bit floating point numbers, this would amount to about
5
terabytes of memory
just to store the matrix of the linear problem
.
Solving the problem typically requires even more memory.

To bypass the above memory requirements, we turn over to a Frank-Wolfe algorithm: Frank-Wolfe algorithms are a family of iterative optimization algorithms.
We first describe in the polytope-membership-problem (PMP) formulation of our inflation problems.
This gives the necessary geometric intuition for the concept of an incompatibility certificate as described in
Section

5.1
,
and we demonstrate how to check such certificates in exact arithmetic, i.e., without suffering from floating-point errors.
Finally, we present the Frank-Wolfe algorithm that we use to find such an incompatibility certificate in
Section

5.2
.

A polytope membership problem.

Using the reduced inflation events
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
(
Eq.

4.37
),
the reduced inflation distributions
\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}
(
Eq.

4.38
)
and the linear map
\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}
(
Eq.

4.46
),
we define the polytope
\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})
as
9
9

9

Note that this polytope is presented as a convex hull of vertices, as opposed to a union of half-spaces: it is thus non-trivial to solve the corresponding membership problem.

\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})=\{\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(q)\;|\;q\in\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}\}=\mathrm{conv}\big(\big\{\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\;\big|\;\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}\big\}\big)\subseteq\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}.

(5.1)

Then, the inflation problem of
Def–.˝

4.3
can be reformulated as the following PMP in the vector space
\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
of
Eq.

4.41
: given
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
,

\displaystyle 0\in\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})

\displaystyle\ \Longleftrightarrow\ p\in\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}\textup{ (inconclusive)},

(5.2)

\displaystyle 0\notin\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})

\displaystyle\ \Longleftrightarrow\ p\notin\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}\implies p\notin\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}\textup{ (incompatible)}.

(5.3)

Thus, whether or not the zero vector
0\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
is part of the polytope
\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})
determines the status of the inflation problem.

5.1
Incompatibility certificates

An incompatibility certificate is, in this context, a feasible solution of the dual linear program that
Def–.˝

4.3
defines, which guarantees that the linear program of
InfCompat

sym3

is infeasible, and thus that
{p\notin\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}}
.
Geometrically, such an incompatibility certificate corresponds to a vector perpendicular to a separating hyperplane between the zero vector
0\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
and the polytope
\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})
as described in
Eq.

5.1
.
See for instance
Fig.

4(d)
for a graphical representation of such a separating hyperplane.

This concept of incompatibility certificate plays a role analogous to a Bell inequality.
A Bell inequality is however more powerful since it can be used to prove that many different distributions are incompatible with the classical Bell scenario.
Bell inequalities have been generalized to network Bell inequalities (or causal compatibility inequalities
[
34
]
).
A netowrk Bell inequality, for the triangle network, is a nonlinear inequalities in the components
p(a,b,c)
such that all
{p\in\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}}
, the inequality is satisfied.
However, the constraints that we impose in the generalized inflation problems that we solve in practice
[
51
,
52
]
, the so-called
linearized polynomial identification
(LPI) constraints
[
12
,
22
,
45
]
, are such that an incompatibility certificate cannot be translated into a network Bell inequality.
While this is a slight drawback, these LPI constraints are extremely useful to obtain tighter inflation relaxations given finite computational resources.

We define the inner product and associated norm of
\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
as: for all
f,f^{\prime}\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
,

\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,f^{\prime}\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}=\sum_{o\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}\hyperlink{target:orbits}{\setminus}\mathchoice{\!}{\!}{}{}{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}}f(o)\cdot f^{\prime}(o),\qquad\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}=\sqrt{\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,f\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}}.

(5.4)

We can now state the incompatibility certificate result that we will use.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
.
If there exists
f\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
such that

\text{for all }\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}:\ \hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}>0,

(5.5)

then it holds that
p\notin\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}
.
We call such a
f
an incompatibility certificate.

Proof.

Suppose that
Eq.

5.5
holds for some
f\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
.
Then, we claim that
p\notin\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}
.
We prove this by contradiction: suppose that
p\in\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}
, so that
there exists
q\in\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}
with
\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(q)=0.

This implies that

\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(q)\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}=0.

(5.6)

Consider now the expansion
q=\sum_{\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}q(\omega)\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega}.

Since
q
is a probability distribution, there exists
\bar{\omega}\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
with
q(\bar{\omega})>0
.
Thus, using first
Eq.

5.6
and then
Eq.

5.5
, we obtain that

0=\textstyle\sum_{\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}}q(\omega)\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}\geq q(\bar{\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\bar{\omega}})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}>0,

(5.7)

a contradiction.
Thus,
Eq.

5.5
implies that
p\notin\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}
.
Using
Proposition

3.2
and the fact that
\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompati}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{expl}}}}=\hyperlink{target:mtinfcompativ}{\textup{{InfCompat}}_{\textup{{sym3}}}}
, this implies that
p\notin\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}
.
∎

Exact arithmetic.

Suppose that the target distribution
p\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:netparties}{\boldsymbol{N}}}}
is such that for all
a,b,c\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}n\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
,

p(a,b,c)=\frac{P(a,b,c)}{D}

(5.8)

where
P(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
D\in\mathbb{N}
.
This is for instance the case of the EJM distribution with
D=256
, see
Eq.

2.8
.
Then, since all we require to verify an incompatibility certificate
f
is the sign of the inner product
\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}
, we can compute instead

D^{2}\cdot|\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|\cdot\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}=\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,D^{2}\cdot|\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|\cdot\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}.

(5.9)

Indeed, it holds that

D^{2}\cdot|\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|\cdot\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}=D^{2}\cdot|\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|\cdot\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\circ\hyperlink{target:inftwirl}{\mathsf{T}}^{{p}}=\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}\circ\big(D^{2}\cdot\hyperlink{target:margmap}{\mathsf{M}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}-P_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot P_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\big)\circ\sum_{\iota\in\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}}\iota.

(5.10)

Notice that this linear map has integer coefficients:
the maps
\hyperlink{target:quovecembed}{\mathsf{E}}^{c,{p}}
and
\hyperlink{target:margmap}{\mathsf{M}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
take sums of coefficients of their inputs,
and the symmetry action of
\iota\in\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}
simply shuffles the coefficients of its input.
Furthermore, in
Eq.

5.9
, the input of this map is a deterministic distribution
\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega}
that has integer coefficients (all
0
’s except a single
1
).
Thus,
D^{2}\cdot|\hyperlink{target:distrgroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{{p}}|\cdot\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})
is a vector with integer coefficients.
Overall, we see that if we choose
f
to be such that
f(o)\in\mathbb{Z}
, we can check the sign of
\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}
using only integer arithmetic.
Provided that integer overflows are appropriately prevented
[
51
,
52
]
, this allows to verify that a candidate incompatibility certificate
f\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}
is indeed valid in exact arithmetic, i.e., without suffering from any floating-point error.
An incompatibility certificate whose validity was verified in that way is thus considered a
computer-assisted proof
of classical causal incompatibility.

(a)

Setup:
v_{i}=\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega_{i}})\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}

(b)

First step:
\mathcal{S}=\{v_{0}\}
,
f_{\textup{p}}=v_{0}
,
s_{\textup{global}}\leq 0

(c)

Second step:
\mathcal{S}=\{v_{0},v_{3}\}
,
s_{\textup{global}}\leq 0

(d)

Third step:
\mathcal{S}=\{v_{0},v_{3},v_{4}\}
,
s_{\textup{global}}>0
,

f_{\textup{p}}
is an incompatibility certificate

.

and gap
s_{\textup{global}}\in\mathbb{Z}
after completing

of

.

5.2
Frank-Wolfe algorithm

Frank-Wolfe algorithms, also known as conditional gradient algorithms
[
49
]
, are iterative algorithms typically used for minimizing a convex function over a convex feasible region by keeping track of the current iterate as an explicit convex mixture of feasible points.
Frank-Wolfe algorithms have been successfully used in the context of Bell nonlocality already
[
48
,
50
]
, and we now show their usefulness for solving inflation problems.

Frank-Wolfe algorithms are particularly well-suited to iteratively find the minimum norm of a vector in a convex set
\mathrm{conv}(\mathcal{S})
that is represented by a finite set of vertices
\mathcal{S}=\{v_{i}\}_{i}
.
In our case, we take
v_{i}=\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega_{i}})
, where
\{\omega_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
.
We define the problem of finding the vector
f_{\textup{p}}
with minimum norm as
10
10

10

This problem is well-defined since the minimizer is unique, see
.

f_{\textup{p}}=\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MinNorm}}}(\mathcal{S})=\argmin_{f\in\mathrm{conv}(\mathcal{S})}\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}.

(5.11)

On the other hand, to help us find an incompatibility certificate as in
Proposition

5.1
, we would like to find a separating hyperplane between the zero vector and
\mathrm{conv}(\mathcal{S})
.
Such a hyperplane, if it exists, can be described with a normal direction
f
, with
\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}\;=1
, and gap
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
s>0
, such that any vertex
v_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
satisfies

s\leq\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,v_{i}\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}.

The problem of finding the “best” separating hyperplane, i.e., the normal direction
f_{\textup{d}}
with maximal gap
s
, can then be formulated as
11
11

11

See
for the uniqueness of the maximizer.

\displaystyle(s,f_{\textup{d}})=\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MaxGap}}}(\mathcal{S})=\argmax_{\scalebox{0.85}{$s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},f\in\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}}$}}\quad

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

(5.12)

\displaystyle{s.t.}

\displaystyle\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}\;\leq 1\text{ and }\forall v_{i}\in\mathcal{S}:\ s^{\prime}\leq\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,v_{i}\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}.

This formulation is such that if there does not exist a separating hyperplane, then
s=0
, in which case we set
f_{\textup{d}}=0
.
Consider now the case where
\mathcal{S}=\{\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\}_{\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
, so that
\mathrm{conv}(\mathcal{S})=\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}}
.
If the solution
(s,f_{\textup{d}})=\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MaxGap}}}(\mathcal{S})
is such that
s>0
, then
f_{\textup{d}}
is an incompatibility certificate in the sense of
Proposition

5.1
(and, in some sense, the best such certificate).
Relatedly, if the solution
f_{\textup{p}}=\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MinNorm}}}(\mathcal{S})
is such that
\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f_{\textup{p}}\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}\;>0
, then
0\notin\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})
, and hence there must exist an incompatibility certificate.

The two problems
\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MinNorm}}}(\mathcal{S})
and
\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MaxGap}}}(\mathcal{S})
seem, at first sight, only vaguely related.
However, they are actually equivalent: these two quadratic optimization problems are dual to each other.
Specifically, we show in
that

\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f_{\textup{p}}\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}\;=s\quad\textup{ and }\quad f_{\textup{p}}=\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}f_{\textup{p}}\hyperlink{target:norm}{\lVert}\cdot f_{\textup{d}}.

(5.13)

Thus, suppose that we iteratively solve the problem of minimizing the norm over the polytope
\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:redinfqs}{\tilde{\Delta}}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}^{{p}})
with a Frank-Wolfe algorithm.
By the above duality result, this equivalently means that we are iteratively looking for a separating hyperplane (i.e., an incompatibility certificate).

The version of Frank-Wolfe that we use is presented in
Algorithm

1
.
This is the so-called
fully-corrective
Frank-Wolfe algorithm, which is characterized by the fact that no vertex is ever eliminated from the active set
\mathcal{S}
(cf.
Algorithm

1
), and the fact that the next iterate
f_{\textup{p}}
is obtained by minimizing the norm over the
full
active set
\mathcal{S}
at each iteration (or equivalently, the next iterate
f_{\textup{d}}
is obtained by maximizing the gap between the zero vector and the full active set
\mathcal{S}
).
In practice, to solve the problem
\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MinNorm}}}(\mathcal{S})
, we solve the dual problem
\hyperlink{target:minnormpb}{\textup{{MaxGap}}}(\mathcal{S})
by formulating it as a quadratic problem that we hand over to the Mosek solver
[
53
]
.
This version of the Frank-Wolfe algorithm performs much better than others in our setting since we have relatively few scalar constraints and many scalar variables, see
Eq.

4.53
.
In practice, we noticed that the fully-corrective Frank-Wolfe algorithm takes only about
\dim(\hyperlink{target:quovecspace}{\vphantom{\tilde{V}}\smash{\tilde{V}\kern-0.5pt}}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\hskip 3.0ptc,{p}})
steps before exiting, independently of the exit status (inconclusive or incompatible).
This also means that the size of the set
\mathcal{S}
remains much smaller than the size of
\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
.

The linear optimization oracle (LMO) is the part of the Frank-Wolfe algorithm that has to solve the minimization problem

\min_{\omega\in\hyperlink{target:redinfevents}{\tilde{\Omega}}^{{p}}_{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\hyperlink{target:inner}{\langle}f,\hyperlink{target:totconstraintmap}{\mathsf{K}}^{c,{p}}(\hyperlink{target:detdistr}{\delta}^{\hskip 0.5pt\omega})\hyperlink{target:inner}{\rangle}.

(5.14)

In the literature, heuristic LMOs that do not actually solve the above minimization problem but return an approximate solution are sometimes considered, as they can yield significant computational improvements
[
48
,
49
,
50
]
.
We choose to use an LMO that returns the actual minimum of the minimization problem, since in our case this yields an overall faster resolution of inflation problems.

6
Applications

We now summarize the applications of our methods to prove the classical causal incompatibility of concrete distributions in the triangle network.
Compared to the inflation problem that we presented in
Section

3
, we consider in practice larger inflation graphs and additional marginal inflation constraints
[
51
,
52
]
:
these additional marginal inflation constraints are called linearized polynomial identification (LPI) constraints and have already been proven useful to prove classical causal incompatibility
[
12
,
22
,
45
]
.
The reason is that this provides a tighter characterization of classically compatible distributions.
The methods described in
Sections

4
and
5
straightforwardly extend to these more general inflation problems, but this requires a fair amount of additional bookkeeping that we omit for the sake of clarity.
The complete mathematical treatment of these inflation problems is available in the related doctoral thesis
[
52
]
.

6.1
The shared random bit

We first consider the following family of distributions with
n=2
outcomes per party, parametrized by the visibility
{v\in[0,1]}
:

\forall a,b,c\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}:\ p_{v}(a,b,c)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{v}{2}+\frac{1-v}{8}&\textup{ if }a=b=c,\\
\frac{1-v}{8}&\textup{ else.}\end{aligned}\right.

(6.1)

Thus,
p_{v=1}
corresponds to a shared random bit between Alice, Bob and Charlie, while
p_{v=0}
is the maximally mixed distribution.
This family of distribution is such that there exists a critical visibility
v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}\in[0,1]
such that
p_{v}
is classically compatible with the triangle network for all
v\leq v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}
and
p_{v}
is incompatible for all
v>v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}

[
52
]
.

This family of distribution is relevant for us because it has been thoroughly investigated using inflation problems
[
45
]
.
Specifically, Ref.
[
45
]
provides upper bounds for
v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}
based on inflation problems like those that we consider in this work.
This is an ideal opportunity to verify that our methods and code works as expected.
In
of
, we show how we indeed reproduce the results of Ref.
[
45
]
.
The best (i.e., lowest) upper bound that was known to date for
v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}
was
v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}\lessapprox 37.72\%

[
45
]
, and was obtained in about one hour by solving an inflation problem with size
({z}_{\alpha}=2,{z}_{\beta}=3,{z}_{\gamma}=3)
.
Using the same time on a modern laptop but solving an inflation problem with size
(3,3,4)
, we are able to show, as an exact upper bound, that
v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}<36.629\%
.
Combined with a lower bound obtained by explicitly constructing a suitable classical causal model
[
19
]
, we thus know that

36.21\%\leq v^{*}_{\textup{srb}}<36.629\%.

(6.2)

Figure 5
:

This larger triangle inflation with size
{z}=(2,2,4)
consists of two copies of the
\alpha
and
\beta
sources and four copies of the
\gamma
source, with eight Alices, eight Bobs and four Charlies.

6.2
The Elegant Joint Measurement

In this section, we use the methods of
Sections

4
and
5
to show that the EJM distribution of
Eq.

2.8
is classically incompatible with the triangle network, i.e., that
p_{\textup{ejm}}\notin\hyperlink{target:localset}{\textup{{ClCompat}}}
.

We are able to prove that the EJM distribution is incompatible using the following inflation problem.
Instead of using the
({z}_{\alpha}=2,{z}_{\beta}=2,{z}_{\gamma}=2)
inflation graph of
Fig.

2
, we use the inflation graph of size
(2,2,4)
that is represented in
Fig.

5
.
We show that there does not exist a normalized non-negative distribution
q
over the outcomes of the parties of the inflation graph of
Fig.

5
such that

\displaystyle\forall\zeta\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}:\ \zeta(q)

\displaystyle=q,

(6.3)

\displaystyle q_{\mathrm{A}_{00}\mathrm{B}_{00}\mathrm{C}_{00}\mathrm{A}_{11}\mathrm{A}_{12}\mathrm{A}_{13}\mathrm{B}_{11}\mathrm{B}_{21}\mathrm{B}_{31}\mathrm{C}_{11}}

\displaystyle=p_{\textup{ejm}}\cdot q_{\mathrm{A}_{11}\mathrm{A}_{12}\mathrm{A}_{13}\mathrm{B}_{11}\mathrm{B}_{21}\mathrm{B}_{31}\mathrm{C}_{11}},

\displaystyle q_{\mathrm{A}_{00}\mathrm{B}_{10}\mathrm{B}_{11}\mathrm{B}_{20}\mathrm{B}_{21}\mathrm{B}_{30}\mathrm{B}_{31}\mathrm{C}_{01}\mathrm{C}_{11}}

\displaystyle=(p_{\textup{ejm}})_{\mathrm{A}_{00}}\cdot q_{\mathrm{B}_{10}\mathrm{B}_{11}\mathrm{B}_{20}\mathrm{B}_{21}\mathrm{B}_{30}\mathrm{B}_{31}\mathrm{C}_{01}\mathrm{C}_{11}},

\displaystyle q_{\mathrm{B}_{00}\mathrm{A}_{01}\mathrm{A}_{11}\mathrm{A}_{02}\mathrm{A}_{12}\mathrm{A}_{03}\mathrm{A}_{13}\mathrm{C}_{10}\mathrm{C}_{11}}

\displaystyle=(p_{\textup{ejm}})_{\mathrm{B}_{00}}\cdot q_{\mathrm{A}_{01}\mathrm{A}_{11}\mathrm{A}_{02}\mathrm{A}_{12}\mathrm{A}_{03}\mathrm{A}_{13}\mathrm{C}_{10}\mathrm{C}_{11}}.

Finding the incompatibility certificate takes about five days on the ETH Zürich Euler cluster using twelve cores and about fifty gigabytes of memory.
Checking that the incompatibility certificate is indeed valid takes about twenty seconds on a modern laptop.
The public repository that gives access to our code also gives access to the certificate and enables the user to verify its validity
[
51
]
.
More details about different inflation problems that we tried are described in
and in particular
and
.

7
Conclusion

The conjecture of classical causal incompatibility of the EJM distribution with the triangle network was formulated eight years ago
[
42
]
.
The fundamental and simple flavor of this conjecture led to numerous attempts to resolve it, whether published
[
42
,
43
,
21
,
22
,
20
]
or unpublished.
These efforts played an important role in the improvements of the tools and ideas available to the community to tackle related problems.
Eventually, the techniques for certifying causal incompatibility improved to the extent that we could resolve the conjecture.
This demonstrates that the inflation technique, and in particular, our implementation thereof
[
20
]
, is the state-of-the-art for characterizing the limits of classical networks.
This also means that inflation is a key technique for understanding quantum-classical gaps in networks and for classical causal inference.

The main insights drawn from our results are as follows.
Due to the fanout nature of the inflation graphs characterizing the classical limits of causal networks, our work reinforces the claim that classical systems are best characterized and conceptualized as systems whose state can be copied.
Furthermore, our symmetry reduction techniques can be seen as yet another example of the usefulness of symmetry in the resolution of physical and mathematical problems.
In particular, in our setting, the ability to copy the classical systems implies the existence of a large symmetry group over the inflation network: there is thus, in a sense, a hidden symmetry group in the classical triangle network that characterizes the compatible distributions.

Let us now mention interesting directions for future research.
Our experience suggests that it remains difficult to obtain a proof of classical causal incompatibility of noisy EJM distributions (e.g., coming from experimental implementations of the triangle network).
Indeed, adding noise on the EJM distribution brings it closer to a distribution that is classically compatible with the triangle network.
A larger inflation problem (a larger inflation graph and more inflation constraints) would be required to prove the incompatibility of the resulting noisy distribution, assuming the noise did not turn it into a compatible distribution.
Solving this larger inflation problem would require significantly more computational resources (i.e., time and memory) than those that we had access to, or further theoretical and algorithmic developments.
Furthermore, if the noisy distribution has fewer symmetries, then the performance boost offered by our symmetry reduction techniques diminishes (although it does not vanish, since the symmetries of the inflation graph will always be exploited).

An interesting research avenue would be to attempt analytical (i.e., pen-and-paper) proofs of causal incompatibility based on inflation relaxations.
For instance, consider an analytical argument of the following type: suppose that the EJM distribution admitted a classical causal model in the triangle network.
Then, the output of the classical sources could be copied and sent to copies of Alice, Bob and Charlie.
The strong, symmetric correlations within the original network would somehow have to be shared with the new copies of the parties.
Somewhere along the line, there would be an impossible step, where the statistics of the copies of the parties would have to obey incompatible constraints (e.g., correlation and anti-correlation, high entropy and low entropy, etc.) originating from considering different part of the inflation network.
We do not know how to make such an argument go through at the time of writing, but the incompatibility of the EJM with inflation relaxations implies that such an argument exists.
It is not clear how simple this argument can be made, and whether it could be completely checked with pen-and-paper, but exploring this direction would bring much more intuition to the proof of causal incompatibility, and therefore, to the concrete differences exhibited by classical and quantum theories in causal networks.

Code availability

We provide the code accompanying this work and implementing our inflation problem solver as an open-source repository
[
51
]
.
The repository furthermore includes a comprehensive documentation of the code.

Appendix A
Symmetry reduction

In this section, we prove the symmetry reduction results that we presented in
Section

4
.
Analogous results can be derived for more general triangle inflation problems featuring an arbitrary inflation size
({z}_{\alpha},{z}_{\beta},{z}_{\gamma})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
as well as more general marginal constraints
[
52
]
.

A.1
Inflation symmetries

A.1.1
Signatures

We denote the elements of
\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{3}
with
\{\mathrm{id},(01),(02),(12),(012),(210)\}
according to the cycle notation.
For instance,
(210)
means that
(210)(2)=1
,
(210)(1)=0
,
(210)(0)=2
.
For

i,j\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
, we will denote addition modulo
3
as follows:

\displaystyle i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}j

\displaystyle=i+j\mod 3,

(A.1)

\displaystyle i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}j

\displaystyle=i-j\mod 3.

For instance,
2\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1=0
,
2\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2=1
,
2\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}3=2
, etc.
With this convention for addition modulo
3
, this means that
(210)(i)=i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2=i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}1
, while
(012)(i)=i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1=i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}2
.
The signature of a permutation
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{3}
is denoted
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)
and can be defined as

\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=\left\{\begin{aligned} +1&\textup{ if }\tau\in\{\mathrm{id},(012),(210)\},\\
-1&\textup{ if }\tau\in\{(01),(02),(12)\}.\end{aligned}\right.

(A.2)

The following lemma will be useful to simplify calculations with
\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{3}
.

Lemma A.1
.

For all
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{3}
, for all
t,l\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
, it holds that

\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}l)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}l&\textup{ if }\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=+1,\\
\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}l&\textup{ if }\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=-1.\end{aligned}\right.

(A.3)

In particular, if
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=-1
, we have
\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1)=\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}1=\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2
and
\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2)=\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}2=\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1
.

Proof.

The statement is clear for the case
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=+1
, since in that case,
\tau
acts as
\tau(i)=i\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}i_{0}
for some
i_{0}\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
.
In the other case where
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=-1
, there exists
i_{0}\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
such that
i_{0}
is the fixed point of
\tau
, and
\tau
exchanges the other two values, i.e.,

\displaystyle\tau(i_{0})

\displaystyle=i_{0},

(A.4)

\displaystyle\tau(i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1)

\displaystyle=i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2,

\displaystyle\tau(i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2)

\displaystyle=i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1.

This can be compactly stated as: for all
i\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
,
\tau(i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}i)=i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}i.

Thus, we also have

\displaystyle\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}l)

\displaystyle=\tau(i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}l\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}i_{0})

\displaystyle=i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}l\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}i_{0}

\displaystyle=i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}l

\displaystyle=\tau(i_{0}\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}i_{0})\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}l

\displaystyle=\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\ominus}l.\qed

A.1.2
Commutation relations: source, party and outcome symmetries

To study commutation relations between source and party symmetries, we first need to define the action of a party symmetry
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}
on a source symmetry
\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\zeta_{1})\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
:

\tau[\zeta]=(\zeta_{\tau^{-1}(0)},\zeta_{\tau^{-1}(1)},\zeta_{\tau^{-1}(2)})\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}.

(A.5)

Importantly, this group action has the following property.

Lemma A.2
.

For all
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}
, for all
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
, it holds that

\tau[\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\prime}]=\tau[\zeta]\cdot\tau[\zeta^{\prime}].

(A.6)

In particular, since
\tau[\mathrm{id}]=\mathrm{id}\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
, this implies that
\tau[\zeta^{-1}]=(\tau[\zeta])^{-1}
.

Proof.

This follows from the group composition of
SrcGrp
: both sides of
Eq.

A.6
are given explicitly by

\big(\zeta_{\tau^{-1}(0)}\circ\zeta^{\prime}_{\tau^{-1}(0)},\ \zeta_{\tau^{-1}(1)}\circ\zeta^{\prime}_{\tau^{-1}(1)},\ \zeta_{\tau^{-1}(2)}\circ\zeta^{\prime}_{\tau^{-1}(2)}\big)\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}.\qed

In the following proof, we will use that the action of a source symmetry
\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
on an inflation party
(t,j,k)\in{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
(see
Eq.

4.2
) as defined in
Eq.

3.7
can be rewritten as

\zeta(t,j,k)=(t,\zeta_{t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1}(j),\zeta_{t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2}(k)),

(A.7)

where
t\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
and
j,k\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}2\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
.

Lemma A.3

(Source and party symmetry commutation)
.

For each party symmetry
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}
, for each source symmetry
\zeta\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
,
we let
\zeta^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime\prime}\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
be (see
Eq.

A.5
)

\zeta^{\prime}=\tau[\zeta],\quad\zeta^{\prime\prime}=\tau^{-1}[\zeta].

(A.8)

Then, for each inflation party
A\in{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\tau\circ\zeta(A)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}\circ\tau(A)\in{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}},

(A.9)

\displaystyle\zeta\circ\tau(A)

\displaystyle=\tau\circ\zeta^{\prime\prime}(A)\in{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}.

(A.10)

It follows from
Eqs.

3.8
and
4.6
that, for each inflation event
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,

\displaystyle\tau\circ\zeta(\omega)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}\circ\tau(\omega)\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

(A.11)

\displaystyle\zeta\circ\tau(\omega)

\displaystyle=\tau\circ\zeta^{\prime\prime}(\omega)\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

(A.12)

and from
Eqs.

3.10
and
4.8
that, for each inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,

\displaystyle\tau\circ\zeta(q)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}\circ\tau(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

(A.13)

\displaystyle\zeta\circ\tau(q)

\displaystyle=\tau\circ\zeta^{\prime\prime}(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

(A.14)

Proof.

Since
Eq.

A.10
follows from
Eq.

A.9
, it if sufficient to prove
Eq.

A.9
.
Instead of
Eq.

A.9
, we prove the equivalent statement (after replacing
\tau
with
\tau^{-1}
and
A
with
\tau(A)
),

\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta\circ\tau(A)=\zeta^{\prime}(A)

(A.15)

where we defined
\zeta^{\prime}=(\zeta^{\prime}_{0},\zeta^{\prime}_{1},\zeta^{\prime}_{2})=\tau^{-1}[\zeta]\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
— recall that this means that

\zeta^{\prime}_{t}=\zeta_{\tau(t)}.

(A.16)

For all
A=(t,j,k)\in{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}
, we let
A^{\prime}=\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta\circ\tau(A)
,
and we will show that
A^{\prime}=\zeta^{\prime}(A)
, which proves
Eq.

A.15
.
Let us consider the possible values for
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)
and make use of
Lemma

A.1
.

—

Assume that
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=+1
, so that

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta\circ\tau(A)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta(\tau(t),j,k)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(\tau(t),\zeta_{\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1}(j),\zeta_{\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2}(k))

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(\tau(t),\zeta_{\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1)}(j),\zeta_{\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2)}(k))

\displaystyle=(t,\zeta_{\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1)}(j),\zeta_{\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2)}(k))

\displaystyle=(t,\zeta^{\prime}_{t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1}(j),\zeta^{\prime}_{t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2}(k))=\zeta^{\prime}(A).

(A.17)

—

Assume now that
\hyperlink{target:sgn}{\mathrm{sgn}}(\tau)=-1
, so that

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta\circ\tau(A)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta(\tau(t),k,j)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}(\tau(t),\zeta_{\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1}(k),\zeta_{\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2}(j))

\displaystyle=(t,\zeta_{\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2}(j),\zeta_{\tau(t)\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1}(k))

\displaystyle=(t,\zeta_{\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1)}(j),\zeta_{\tau(t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2)}(k))

\displaystyle=(t,\zeta^{\prime}_{t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}1}(j),\zeta^{\prime}_{t\hyperlink{target:mplus}{\oplus}2}(k))=\zeta^{\prime}(A).\qed

The following commutation relations are straightforward to prove: in short, outcome symmetries commute with all other types of symmetries.

Lemma A.4

(Commutation relations with outcome symmetries)
.

For each source symmetry
\zeta\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
,
for each party symmetry
\tau\in\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}
,
for each outcome symmetry
\sigma\in\hyperlink{target:outgroup}{\textup{{OutGrp}}}
,
for each inflation event
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,
for each inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\tau\circ\sigma(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\tau(\omega)\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

(A.18)

\displaystyle\zeta\circ\sigma(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\zeta(\omega)\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

\displaystyle\tau\circ\sigma(q)

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\tau(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}},

\displaystyle\zeta\circ\sigma(q)

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\zeta(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}.

A.1.3
Inflation symmetry group

We defined the inflation symmetry group as
\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}=\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}\times\hyperlink{target:party group}{\textup{{PartyGrp}}}\times\hyperlink{target:outgroup}{\textup{{OutGrp}}}
.
The group operation is defined as follows: for all
{{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}},{{(\zeta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}
,

{{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\cdot{{(\zeta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}}={\Big(\zeta\cdot\tau[\zeta^{\prime}],\tau\circ\tau^{\prime},\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}\Big)}.

(A.19)

It is straightforward to show that, for all
\iota={{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}
,

\iota^{-1}={\Big(\tau^{-1}[\zeta^{-1}],\tau^{-1},\sigma^{-1}\Big)}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}.

(A.20)

We can confirm that the group operation is well-defined by checking its associativity:
let
{(\zeta_{i},\tau_{i},\sigma_{i})}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}
for
i\in\hyperlink{target:ints}{[\![}3\hyperlink{target:ints}{]\!]}
.
We want to show that

\Big({(\zeta_{0},\tau_{0},\sigma_{0})}\cdot{(\zeta_{1},\tau_{1},\sigma_{1})}\Big)\cdot{(\zeta_{2},\tau_{2},\sigma_{2})}={(\zeta_{0},\tau_{0},\sigma_{0})}\cdot\Big({(\zeta_{1},\tau_{1},\sigma_{1})}\cdot{(\zeta_{2},\tau_{2},\sigma_{2})}\Big).

(A.21)

The left-hand (
L
) and right-hand (
R
) side equal

{(\zeta_{L/R},\tau_{0}\circ\tau_{1}\circ\tau_{2},\sigma_{0}\circ\sigma_{1}\circ\sigma_{2})},

(A.22)

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{L}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{0}\cdot\tau_{0}[\zeta_{1}]\cdot\big(\tau_{0}\circ\tau_{1}[\zeta_{2}]\big)

(A.23)

\displaystyle\zeta_{R}

\displaystyle=\zeta_{0}\cdot\tau_{0}\big[\zeta_{1}\cdot\tau_{1}[\zeta_{2}]\big].

Using
Lemma

A.2
and the group action property
\tau_{0}\circ\tau_{1}[\zeta_{2}]=\tau_{0}\big[\tau_{1}[\zeta_{2}]\big]
, it follows that

\zeta_{R}=\zeta_{0}\cdot\tau_{0}[\zeta_{1}]\cdot\tau_{0}\big[\tau_{1}[\zeta_{2}]\big]=\zeta_{L}.

(A.24)

Furthermore, the group operation is such that the group actions are sensible.
Consider for instance the action of
{{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}},{{(\zeta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}}\in\hyperlink{target:fullgroup}{\textup{{InfGrp}}}
on an inflation event
\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
(
Eq.

4.14
).
Letting first
{{(\zeta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}}
act on
\omega
, followed by the action of
{{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}
, we get

\displaystyle{{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\circ{{(\zeta^{\prime},\tau^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})}}(\omega)

\displaystyle={{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\big(\sigma^{\prime}\circ\omega\circ\tau^{\prime-1}\circ\zeta^{\prime-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\omega\circ\tau^{\prime-1}\circ\zeta^{\prime-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\omega\circ\tau^{\prime-1}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\tau[\zeta^{\prime-1}]\circ\zeta^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime})\circ\omega\circ(\tau\circ\tau^{\prime})^{-1}\circ(\zeta\circ\tau[\zeta^{\prime}])^{-1},

(A.25)

which is exactly the action of the right-hand side of
Eq.

A.19
on the inflation event
\omega
.
Note that we used
Lemmas

A.2
and
A.3
.

A.1.4
Commutation relations: inflation symmetries and constraint map

Recall the definition of the constraint symmetry group
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
in
Eq.

4.24
.
We can show that all constraint symmetries commute with the constraint map
\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}
of
Eq.

3.16
, which we can symbolically write as
[{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}},\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}]=0
.
This is the crucial ingredient for our upcoming symmetrization results.

Lemma A.5

(Constraint symmetry commutation)
.

For each constraint symmetry
\iota={{(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)}}\in{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
,
for each inflation distribution
q\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
,
it holds that

\iota\big(\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}(q)\big)=\hyperlink{target:constraintmap}{\mathsf{L}}^{c,{p}}\big(\iota(q)\big).

(A.26)

Proof.

Equation

A.26
is equivalent to

\iota(q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})-\iota(p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})=[\iota(q)]_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}-p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}},

(A.27)

where we used that
\iota(q)\in\hyperlink{target:distrs}{\Delta}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
, and is thus appropriately normalized, to simplify the second term of the right-hand side.

We start by proving that

\iota(q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})=[\iota(q)]_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(A.28)

For each marginal inflation event
\mu\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
,
using
Eq.

3.12
,

[\iota(q)]_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}(\mu)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\\
\omega_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\mu\end{subarray}}\big[\iota(q)\big](\omega)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\\
\omega_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\mu\end{subarray}}q\big(\iota^{-1}(\omega)\big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega^{\prime}\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\\
\iota(\omega^{\prime})_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\mu\end{subarray}}q(\omega^{\prime})\\[10.0pt]
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega^{\prime}\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}\\
\omega^{\prime}_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\iota^{-1}(\mu)\end{subarray}}q(\omega^{\prime})=\big[q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}\big]\big(\iota^{-1}(\mu)\big)=\big[\iota(q_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})\big](\mu),

(A.29)

where we defined
\omega^{\prime}=\iota^{-1}(\omega)\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\hyperlink{target:infparties}{\boldsymbol{I}}}}
and we used that

\iota(\omega^{\prime})_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\mu\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \omega^{\prime}_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\iota^{-1}(\mu),

(A.30)

or more explicitly,

\sigma\circ\omega^{\prime}\circ\tau^{-1}\circ\zeta^{-1}_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\mu\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \omega^{\prime}_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}=\sigma^{-1}\circ\mu\circ\zeta\circ\tau_{|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}^{\,},

(A.31)

which relies on the fact that
\tau({\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1})=\zeta({\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1})={\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
, which follows from the definition on
{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
(
Eq.

4.24
) and the fact that
(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)\in{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
.

We finish by showing that

\iota(p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}})=p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}}\cdot p_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}.

(A.32)

We will identify a marginal inflation event
\mu\in\hyperlink{target:def events}{\Omega}_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}}
with the list of outcomes

\mu\simeq\big(\mu(\mathrm{A}_{00}),\mu(\mathrm{B}_{00}),\mu(\mathrm{C}_{00}),\mu(\mathrm{A}_{11}),\mu(\mathrm{B}_{11}),\mu(\mathrm{C}_{11})\big)=\big(a_{0}^{0},a_{0}^{1},a_{0}^{2},a_{1}^{0},a_{1}^{1},a_{1}^{2}\big),

(A.33)

i.e., in the notation of
Eq.

4.2
, the party
(t,j,j)\in{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{A}}_{1}
gets the outcome
\mu(t,j,j)=a_{j}^{t}
.
Then, the constraint symmetry
\iota=(\zeta,\tau,\sigma)\in{\hyperlink{target:marggroup}{\mathbb{G}}^{c,{p}}}
with
\zeta=(\zeta_{0},\zeta_{0},\zeta_{0})\in\hyperlink{target:sourcegroup}{\textup{{SrcGrp}}}
and
\zeta_{0}\in\hyperlink{target:shortsymgroup}{S}_{2}
acts on
\mu
as

\displaystyle\iota^{-1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-1}\circ\mu\circ\zeta\circ\tau

\displaystyle\simeq\Big(\sigma^{-1}\big(a_{\zeta_{0}(0)}^{\tau(0)}\big),\sigma^{-1}\big(a_{\zeta_{0}(0)}^{\tau(1)}\big),\sigma^{-1}\big(a_{\zeta_{0}(0)}^{\tau(2)}\big),\sigma^{-1}\big(a_{\zeta_{0}(1)}^{\tau(0)}\big),\sigma^{-1}\big(a_{\zeta_{0}(1)}^{\tau(1)}\big),\sigma^{-1}\big(a_{\zeta_{0}(1)}^{\tau(2)}\big)\Big).

(A.34)

Starting from the left-hand side of
Eq.

A.32
, it holds that
